  (function() {
    var header = document.getElementById("__rptl-header"),
        burgerState = document.getElementById("__rptl-header-burger-state"),
        cookiebanner = document.getElementById("__rptl-cookiebanner"),
        accept = document.getElementById("__rptl-cookiebanner-accept"),
        reject = document.getElementById("__rptl-cookiebanner-reject");

    if (document.cookie.indexOf("cookiebanner_accepted=") === -1) {
      cookiebanner.classList.remove("__rptl-cookiebanner-hidden");
    }

    accept.addEventListener("click", function (e) {
      document.cookie = "cookiebanner_accepted=1; path=/; max-age=" + 60 * 60 * 24 * 365 * 2;

      if (typeof window.gtag !== "undefined") {
        window.gtag("consent", "update", {
          analytics_storage: "granted",
        });
      }

      cookiebanner.classList.add("__rptl-cookiebanner-hidden");
    });

    reject.addEventListener("click", function (e) {
      document.cookie = "cookiebanner_accepted=0; path=/; max-age=" + 60 * 60 * 24 * 365 * 2;

      cookiebanner.classList.add("__rptl-cookiebanner-hidden");
    });

    document.addEventListener("click", function (e) {
      if (!header.contains(e.target)) {
        burgerState.checked = false;
      }
    });
  })();

      <div id="docs-container">
        <div id="main-window">
          <section id="content" role="main"><h1><code class="language-plaintext highlighter-rouge">config.txt</code>
</h1>
            

<div class="sect1">
<h2 id="what-is-config-txt">
<a class="anchor" href="#what-is-config-txt"></a>What is <code>config.txt</code>?</h2>
<div class="sectionbody"></div>
<div class="paragraph edit-link">
<p>Edit this <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/documentation/blob/master/documentation/asciidoc/computers/config_txt/what_is_config_txt.adoc">on GitHub</a></p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Instead of the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BIOS">BIOS</a> found on a conventional PC, Raspberry Pi devices use a configuration file called <code>config.txt</code>. The GPU reads <code>config.txt</code> before the Arm CPU and Linux initialise. Raspberry Pi OS looks for this file in the <strong>boot partition</strong>, located at <code>/boot/firmware/</code>.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>You can edit <code>config.txt</code> directly from your Raspberry Pi OS installation. You can also remove the storage device and edit files in the boot partition, including <code>config.txt</code>, from a separate computer.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Changes to <code>config.txt</code> only take effect after a reboot. You can view the current active settings using the following commands:</p>
</div>
<div class="dlist">
<dl><dt class="hdlist1"><code>vcgencmd get_config &lt;config&gt;</code></dt>
<dd>
<p>displays a specific config value, for example, <code>vcgencmd get_config arm_freq</code></p>
</dd>
<dt class="hdlist1"><code>vcgencmd get_config int</code></dt>
<dd>
<p>lists all non-zero integer config options (non-zero)</p>
</dd>
<dt class="hdlist1"><code>vcgencmd get_config str</code></dt>
<dd>
<p>lists all non-null string config options</p>
</dd>
</dl></div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
Not all config settings can be retrieved using <code>vcgencmd</code>.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Some legacy <code>config.txt</code> options are no longer officially supported. These are listed in <a href="../computers/legacy_config_txt.html">Legacy config.txt options</a> and aren’t included in this article.</p>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="file-format">
<a class="anchor" href="#file-format"></a>File format</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>config.txt</code> file is read by the early-stage boot firmware, so it uses a very simple file format: <strong>a single <code>property=value</code> statement on each line, where <code>value</code> is either an integer or a string</strong>. You can add comments, or comment out existing config values, by starting a line with the <code>#</code> character.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>There is a 98-character line length limit for entries. Raspberry Pi OS ignores any characters past this limit.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Here is an example file:</p>
</div>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="ini"><span class="c"># Enable audio (loads snd_bcm2835)
</span><span class="py">dtparam</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">audio=on</span>
<span class="w">
</span><span class="c"># Automatically load overlays for detected cameras
</span><span class="py">camera_auto_detect</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">1</span>
<span class="w">
</span><span class="c"># Automatically load overlays for detected DSI displays
</span><span class="py">display_auto_detect</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">1</span>
<span class="w">
</span><span class="c"># Enable DRM VC4 V3D driver
</span><span class="py">dtoverlay</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">vc4-kms-v3d</span></code></pre>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="advanced-features">
<a class="anchor" href="#advanced-features"></a>Advanced features</h3>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="include">
<a class="anchor" href="#include"></a><code>include</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Causes the content of the specified file to be inserted into the current file.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>For example, adding the line <code>include extraconfig.txt</code> to <code>config.txt</code> will include the content of <code>extraconfig.txt</code> file in the <code>config.txt</code> file.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>bootcode.bin</code> or EEPROM bootloaders do not support the <code>include</code> directive.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Settings which are handled by the bootloader will only take effect if they are specified in <code>config.txt</code> (rather than any additional included file):</p>
</div>
<div class="ulist">
<ul><li>
<p><code>bootcode_delay</code>,</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>gpu_mem</code>, <code>gpu_mem_256</code>, <code>gpu_mem_512</code>, <code>gpu_mem_1024</code>,</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>total_mem</code>,</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>sdram_freq</code>,</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>start_x</code>, <code>start_debug</code>, <code>start_file</code>, <code>fixup_file</code>,</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>uart_2ndstage</code>.</p>
</li>
</ul></div>
</td>
</tr></table></div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="conditional-filtering">
<a class="anchor" href="#conditional-filtering"></a>Conditional filtering</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Conditional filters are covered in the <a href="#conditional-filters">conditionals section</a>.</p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect1">
<h2 id="autoboot-txt">
<a class="anchor" href="#autoboot-txt"></a><code>autoboot.txt</code>
</h2>
<div class="sectionbody"></div>
<div class="paragraph edit-link">
<p>Edit this <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/documentation/blob/master/documentation/asciidoc/computers/config_txt/autoboot.adoc">on GitHub</a></p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>autoboot.txt</code> is an optional configuration file placed in the initial boot partition that can be used to specify the <code>boot_partition</code> number.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>This file can be used in conjunction with the <code>tryboot</code> feature to implement A/B booting for fail-safe operating system upgrades. It is a native bootloader and firmware capability that operates independently of third-party multi-boot managers.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
The <code>autoboot.txt</code> file only controls which boot partition the bootloader selects. Building a resilient, production-ready A/B update system requires dedicated partition layouts (such as redundant boot and root filesystems) and update tooling. For an automated solution, see <a href="../services/connect.html#remoteupdate-intro">A/B Boot Update</a> and the <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/rpi-image-gen">rpi-image-gen</a> tool.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>autoboot.txt</code> file supports the <code>[all]</code>, <code>[none]</code>, and <code>[tryboot]</code> <a href="../computers/config_txt.html#conditional-filters">conditional filters</a>.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>See also the <a href="raspberry-pi.html#fail-safe-os-updates-tryboot">TRYBOOT</a> boot flow.</p>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="boot_partition">
<a class="anchor" href="#boot_partition"></a><code>boot_partition</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Specifies the partition number for booting unless the partition number was already specified as a parameter to the <code>reboot</code> command (for example, <code>sudo reboot 2</code>).</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Partition numbers start at <code>1</code>. Specifying partition <code>0</code> instructs the bootloader to pick the default partition, which resolves to the first bootable FAT partition (normally partition 1).</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Bootable partitions must be formatted as FAT12, FAT16, or FAT32 and contain a <code>start.elf</code> file (or a <code>config.txt</code> file on Raspberry Pi 5) in order for the bootloader to classify them as bootable.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="connection-to-cmdline-txt-and-root-filesystem-selection">
<a class="anchor" href="#connection-to-cmdline-txt-and-root-filesystem-selection"></a>Connection to <code>cmdline.txt</code> and root filesystem selection</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The bootloader only reads <code>autoboot.txt</code> to locate the target boot partition. It doesn’t parse or act on <code>cmdline.txt</code> beyond passing its arguments to the operating system kernel.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Root partition selection (<code>root=…​</code>) is handled by the kernel through <code>cmdline.txt</code>. In a dual-slot A/B system, each boot partition typically contains its own independent <code>cmdline.txt</code> file pointing to its corresponding root partition. Alternatively, if identical files are used on each boot partition, you can use the <code>[boot_partition=N]</code> conditional filter in <code>config.txt</code> to select an alternate <code>cmdline.txt</code>:</p>
</div>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="ini"><span class="nn">[boot_partition=2]</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="py">cmdline</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">cmdline_rootfs_a.txt</span>
<span class="w">
</span><span class="nn">[boot_partition=3]</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="py">cmdline</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">cmdline_rootfs_b.txt</span></code></pre>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="tryboot_a_b">
<a class="anchor" href="#tryboot_a_b"></a><code>tryboot_a_b</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Set this property to <code>1</code> to load the normal <code>config.txt</code> and <code>boot.img</code> files instead of <code>tryboot.txt</code> and <code>tryboot.img</code> when the <code>tryboot</code> flag is set.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>This enables the <code>tryboot</code> switch to be made at the partition level rather than the file level, without having to modify configuration files in the A/B partitions.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="example-update-flow-for-ab-booting">
<a class="anchor" href="#example-update-flow-for-ab-booting"></a>Example update flow for A/B booting</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Consider an A/B partition layout such as the following:</p>
</div>
<div class="ulist">
<ul><li>
<p><strong>Partition 1 (FAT):</strong> Initial bootloader partition containing <code>autoboot.txt</code>.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Partition 2 (FAT):</strong> Slot A boot partition (<code>boot_partition=2</code>), containing firmware, kernel, and a <code>cmdline.txt</code> pointing to Root A.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Partition 3 (FAT):</strong> Slot B boot partition (<code>boot_partition=3</code>), containing firmware, kernel, and a <code>cmdline.txt</code> pointing to Root B.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Partition 4:</strong> Slot A root filesystem partition (Root A).</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Partition 5:</strong> Slot B root filesystem partition (Root B).</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Partition 6:</strong> An optional persistent data partition.</p>
</li>
</ul></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Initial <code>autoboot.txt</code>:</p>
</div>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="ini"><span class="nn">[all]</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="py">tryboot_a_b</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">1</span>
<span class="py">boot_partition</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">2</span>
<span class="nn">[tryboot]</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="py">boot_partition</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">3</span></code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="installing-the-update">
<a class="anchor" href="#installing-the-update"></a>Installing the update</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The system boots into partition 2 by default. A hypothetical update service then:</p>
</div>
<div class="olist arabic">
<ol class="arabic"><li>
<p>Installs the next version of the OS into partition 3 (and its corresponding root partition).</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>Tests the update by executing a tryboot reboot:</p>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="console"><span class="gp">$</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nb">sudo </span>reboot <span class="s2">"0 tryboot"</span></code></pre>
</div>
</div>
</li>
</ol></div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="committing-or-cancelling-the-update">
<a class="anchor" href="#committing-or-cancelling-the-update"></a>Committing or cancelling the update</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The system boots from partition 3 because the <code>[tryboot]</code> filter in <code>autoboot.txt</code> evaluates to true.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If <code>tryboot</code> is active (<code>/proc/device-tree/chosen/bootloader/tryboot</code> equals <code>1</code>) and we’ve booted from the <code>tryboot</code> partition (<code>/proc/device-tree/chosen/bootloader/partition</code> matches the <code>boot_partition</code> set in the <code>[tryboot]</code> section of <code>autoboot.txt</code>):
   * The hypothetical update service would then validate system health.
     * <strong>If successful:</strong> Update <code>autoboot.txt</code> to swap the default and tryboot partitions, committing the update.
     * <strong>If failed:</strong> Perform a normal reboot without altering <code>autoboot.txt</code>. The <code>tryboot</code> flag is automatically cleared, causing the system to revert to booting from partition 2.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Updated <code>autoboot.txt</code> after committing:</p>
</div>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="ini"><span class="nn">[all]</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="py">tryboot_a_b</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">1</span>
<span class="py">boot_partition</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">3</span>
<span class="nn">[tryboot]</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="py">boot_partition</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">2</span></code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock tip">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Tip</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Properties located under <code>/proc/device-tree/chosen/bootloader/</code> (such as <code>tryboot</code> and <code>partition</code>) are stored as raw 32-bit big-endian binary integers. Viewing them with tools like <code>cat</code> produces non-printable characters or appears blank. To read these integer values from a shell script, use:</p>
</div>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="bash"><span class="nb">printf</span> <span class="s2">"%d</span><span class="se">\n</span><span class="s2">"</span> <span class="s2">"0x</span><span class="si">$(</span><span class="nb">od</span> /proc/device-tree/chosen/bootloader/tryboot <span class="nt">-v</span> <span class="nt">-An</span> <span class="nt">-t</span> x1 | <span class="nb">tr</span> <span class="nt">-d</span> <span class="s1">' '</span><span class="si">)</span><span class="s2">"</span></code></pre>
</div>
</div>
</td>
</tr></table></div>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect1">
<h2 id="common-options">
<a class="anchor" href="#common-options"></a>Common options</h2>
<div class="sectionbody"></div>
<div class="paragraph edit-link">
<p>Edit this <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/documentation/blob/master/documentation/asciidoc/computers/config_txt/common.adoc">on GitHub</a></p>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="common-display-options">
<a class="anchor" href="#common-display-options"></a>Common display options</h3>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="hdmi_enable_4kp60">
<a class="anchor" href="#hdmi_enable_4kp60"></a><code>hdmi_enable_4kp60</code>
</h4>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
This option applies only to Raspberry Pi 4B, Compute Module 4, Compute Module 4S, and Raspberry Pi 400.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>By default, when connected to a 4K monitor, certain models select a 30 Hz refresh rate. Use this option to allow selection of 60 Hz refresh rates. Models impacted by this setting only support 4Kp60 output on HDMI0. Enabling this setting increases power consumption and temperature.</p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="common-hardware-configuration-options">
<a class="anchor" href="#common-hardware-configuration-options"></a>Common hardware configuration options</h3>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="camera_auto_detect">
<a class="anchor" href="#camera_auto_detect"></a><code>camera_auto_detect</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>By default, Raspberry Pi OS includes a line in <code>/boot/firmware/config.txt</code> that enables this setting.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>When enabled, the firmware will automatically load overlays for recognised CSI cameras.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>To disable, set <code>camera_auto_detect=0</code> (or remove <code>camera_auto_detect=1</code>).</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="display_auto_detect">
<a class="anchor" href="#display_auto_detect"></a><code>display_auto_detect</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>By default, Raspberry Pi OS includes a line in <code>/boot/firmware/config.txt</code> that enables this setting.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>When enabled, the firmware will automatically load overlays for recognised DSI displays.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>To disable, set <code>display_auto_detect=0</code> (or remove <code>display_auto_detect=1</code>).</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="dtoverlay">
<a class="anchor" href="#dtoverlay"></a><code>dtoverlay</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>dtoverlay</code> option requests the firmware to load a named Device Tree overlay – a configuration file that can enable kernel support for built-in and external hardware. For example, <code>dtoverlay=vc4-kms-v3d</code> loads an overlay that enables the kernel graphics driver.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>As a special case, if called with no value – <code>dtoverlay=</code> – the option marks the end of a list of overlay parameters. If used before any other <code>dtoverlay</code> or <code>dtparam</code> setting, it prevents the loading of any HAT overlay.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>For more details, see <a href="configuration.html#part3.1">DTBs, overlays and config.txt</a>.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="dtparam">
<a class="anchor" href="#dtparam"></a><code>dtparam</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Device Tree configuration files for Raspberry Pi devices support various parameters for such things as enabling I2C and SPI interfaces. Many DT overlays are configurable using parameters. Both types of parameters can be supplied using the <code>dtparam</code> setting. In addition, overlay parameters can be appended to the <code>dtoverlay</code> option, separated by commas, but keep in mind the line length limit of 98 characters.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>For more details, see <a href="configuration.html#part3.1">DTBs, overlays and config.txt</a>.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="arm_boost">
<a class="anchor" href="#arm_boost"></a><code>arm_boost</code>
</h4>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
This option applies only to later Raspberry Pi 4B revisions, which include two-phase power delivery, and all revisions of Raspberry Pi 400.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>By default, Raspberry Pi OS includes a line in <code>/boot/firmware/config.txt</code> that enables this setting on supported devices.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Some Raspberry Pi devices have a second switch-mode power supply for the SoC voltage rail. When enabled, increases the default turbo-mode clock from 1.5 GHz to 1.8 GHz.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>To disable, set <code>arm_boost=0</code>.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="power_force_3v3_pwm">
<a class="anchor" href="#power_force_3v3_pwm"></a><code>power_force_3v3_pwm</code>
</h4>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
This option applies only to Raspberry Pi 5, 500, 500+, and Compute Module 5.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Forces PWM on 3.3V output from the GPIO header or CSI connector.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>To disable, set <code>power_force_3v3_pwm=0</code>.</p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect1">
<h2 id="onboard-analogue-audio-3-5-mm-jack">
<a class="anchor" href="#onboard-analogue-audio-3-5-mm-jack"></a>Onboard analogue audio (3.5 mm jack)</h2>
<div class="sectionbody"></div>
<div class="paragraph edit-link">
<p>Edit this <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/documentation/blob/master/documentation/asciidoc/computers/config_txt/audio.adoc">on GitHub</a></p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The onboard audio output uses config options to change the way the analogue audio is driven, and whether some firmware features are enabled or not.</p>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="audio_pwm_mode">
<a class="anchor" href="#audio_pwm_mode"></a><code>audio_pwm_mode</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>audio_pwm_mode=1</code> selects legacy low-quality analogue audio from the 3.5 mm AV jack.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>audio_pwm_mode=2</code> (the default) selects high quality analogue audio using an advanced modulation scheme.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
This option uses more GPU compute resources and can interfere with some use cases on some models.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="disable_audio_dither">
<a class="anchor" href="#disable_audio_dither"></a><code>disable_audio_dither</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>By default, a 1.0LSB dither is applied to the audio stream if it is routed to the analogue audio output. This can create audible background hiss in some situations, for example when the ALSA volume is set to a low level. Set <code>disable_audio_dither</code> to <code>1</code> to disable dither application.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="enable_audio_dither">
<a class="anchor" href="#enable_audio_dither"></a><code>enable_audio_dither</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Audio dither (see disable_audio_dither above) is normally disabled when the audio samples are larger than 16 bits. Set this option to <code>1</code> to force the use of dithering for all bit depths.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="pwm_sample_bits">
<a class="anchor" href="#pwm_sample_bits"></a><code>pwm_sample_bits</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>pwm_sample_bits</code> command adjusts the bit depth of the analogue audio output. The default bit depth is <code>11</code>. Selecting bit depths below <code>8</code> will result in nonfunctional audio, as settings below <code>8</code> result in a PLL frequency too low to support. This is generally only useful as a demonstration of how bit depth affects quantisation noise.</p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect1">
<h2 id="hdmi-audio">
<a class="anchor" href="#hdmi-audio"></a>HDMI audio</h2>
<div class="sectionbody"></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>By default, HDMI audio output is enabled on all Raspberry Pi models with HDMI output.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>To disable HDMI audio output, append <code>,noaudio</code> to the end of the <code>dtoverlay=vc4-kms-v3d</code> line in <a href="../computers/config_txt.html#what-is-config-txt"><code>/boot/firmware/config.txt</code></a>:</p>
</div>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="ini"><span class="py">dtoverlay</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">vc4-kms-v3d,noaudio</span></code></pre>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect1">
<h2 id="boot-options">
<a class="anchor" href="#boot-options"></a>Boot Options</h2>
<div class="sectionbody"></div>
<div class="paragraph edit-link">
<p>Edit this <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/documentation/blob/master/documentation/asciidoc/computers/config_txt/boot.adoc">on GitHub</a></p>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="start_file-fixup_file">
<a class="anchor" href="#start_file-fixup_file"></a><code>start_file</code>, <code>fixup_file</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>These options specify the firmware files transferred to the VideoCore GPU prior to booting.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>start_file</code> specifies the VideoCore firmware file to use.
<code>fixup_file</code> specifies the file used to fix up memory locations used in the <code>start_file</code> to match the GPU memory split.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>start_file</code> and the <code>fixup_file</code> are a matched pair – using unmatched files will stop the board from booting. This is an advanced option, so we advise that you use <code>start_x</code> and <code>start_debug</code> rather than this option.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
Cut-down firmware (<code>start*cd.elf</code> and <code>fixup*cd.dat</code>) cannot be selected this way – the system will fail to boot.  The only way to enable the cut-down firmware is to specify <code>gpu_mem=16</code>. The cut-down firmware removes support for codecs, 3D and debug logging as well as limiting the initial early-boot framebuffer to 1080p @16bpp – although KMS can replace this with up to 32bpp 4K framebuffer(s) at a later stage as with any firmware.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
The Raspberry Pi 5, 500, 500+, and Compute Module 5 firmware is self-contained in the bootloader EEPROM.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="cmdline">
<a class="anchor" href="#cmdline"></a><code>cmdline</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>cmdline</code> is the alternative filename on the boot partition from which to read the kernel command line string; the default value is <code>cmdline.txt</code>.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="kernel">
<a class="anchor" href="#kernel"></a><code>kernel</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>kernel</code> is the alternative filename on the boot partition for loading the kernel. The default value on the Raspberry Pi 1, Zero and Zero W, and Raspberry Pi Compute Module 1 is <code>kernel.img</code>. The default value on the Raspberry Pi 2, 3, 3+ and Zero 2 W, and Raspberry Pi Compute Modules 3 and 3+ is <code>kernel7.img</code>. The default value on the Raspberry Pi 4 and 400, and Raspberry Pi Compute Module 4 is <code>kernel8.img</code>, or <code>kernel7l.img</code> if <code>arm_64bit</code> is set to 0.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The Raspberry Pi 5, 500, 500+, and Compute Module 5 firmware defaults to loading <code>kernel_2712.img</code> because this image contains optimisations specific to those models (for example, 16K page-size). If this file is not present, then the common 64-bit kernel (<code>kernel8.img</code>) will be loaded instead.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="arm_64bit">
<a class="anchor" href="#arm_64bit"></a><code>arm_64bit</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If set to 1, the kernel will be started in 64-bit mode. Setting to 0 selects 32-bit mode.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>In 64-bit mode, the firmware will choose an appropriate kernel (for example, <code>kernel8.img</code>), unless there is an explicit <code>kernel</code> option defined, in which case that is used instead.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Defaults to 1 on Raspberry Pi 4, 400, and Compute Module 4 and 4S platforms. Defaults to 0 on all other platforms. However, if the name given in an explicit <code>kernel</code> option matches one of the known kernels then <code>arm_64bit</code> is set accordingly.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>64-bit kernels come in the following forms:</p>
</div>
<div class="ulist">
<ul><li>
<p>uncompressed image files</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>gzip archives of an image</p>
</li>
</ul></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Both forms can use the <code>img</code> file extension; the bootloader recognises archives using signature bytes at the start of the file.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The following Raspberry Pi models support this flag:</p>
</div>
<div class="ulist">
<ul><li>
<p>2B rev 1.2</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>3B</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>3A+</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>3B+</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>4B</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>400</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>Zero 2 W</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>Compute Module 3</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>Compute Module 3+</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>Compute Module 4</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>Compute Module 4S</p>
</li>
</ul></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Flagship models since Raspberry Pi 5, Compute Modules since CM5, and Keyboard models since Raspberry Pi 500 <em>only</em> support the 64-bit kernel. Models that only support a 64-bit kernel ignore this flag.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="armstub">
<a class="anchor" href="#armstub"></a><code>armstub</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>armstub</code> is the filename on the boot partition from which to load the Arm stub. The default Arm stub is stored in firmware and is selected automatically based on the Raspberry Pi model and various settings.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The stub is a small piece of Arm code that is run before the kernel. Its job is to set up low-level hardware like the interrupt controller before passing control to the kernel.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="ramfsfile">
<a class="anchor" href="#ramfsfile"></a><code>ramfsfile</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>ramfsfile</code> is the optional filename on the boot partition of a <code>ramfs</code> to load.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
Newer firmware supports the loading of multiple <code>ramfs</code> files. You should separate the multiple file names with commas, taking care not to exceed the 80-character line length limit. All the loaded files are concatenated in memory and treated as a single <code>ramfs</code> blob. More information is available <a href="https://forums.raspberrypi.com/viewtopic.php?f=63&amp;t=10532">on the forums</a>.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="ramfsaddr">
<a class="anchor" href="#ramfsaddr"></a><code>ramfsaddr</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>ramfsaddr</code> is the memory address to which the <code>ramfsfile</code> should be loaded.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="initramfs">
<a class="anchor" href="#initramfs"></a><code>initramfs</code>
</h3>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
Building and updating <code>initramfs</code> is considered an advanced technique that’s undertaken only by experienced users. It isn’t officially supported by Raspberry Pi and we therefore don’t provide instructions for it.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>initramfs</code> command specifies both the ramfs filename <strong>and</strong> the memory address to which to load it. It performs the actions of both <code>ramfsfile</code> and <code>ramfsaddr</code> in one parameter. The address can also be <code>followkernel</code> (or <code>0</code>) to place it in memory after the kernel image. Example values are: <code>initramfs initramf.gz 0x00800000</code> or <code>initramfs init.gz followkernel</code>. As with <code>ramfsfile</code>, newer firmwares allow the loading of multiple files by comma-separating their names.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
This option uses different syntax from all the other options, and you should not use the <code>=</code> character here.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="auto_initramfs">
<a class="anchor" href="#auto_initramfs"></a><code>auto_initramfs</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If <code>auto_initramfs</code> is set to <code>1</code>, the firmware looks for an <code>initramfs</code> file to match the kernel. The file must be in the same location as the kernel image, and the name is derived from the name of the kernel by replacing the <code>kernel</code> prefix with <code>initramfs</code>, and removing any extension such as <code>.img</code>. For example, <code>kernel8.img</code> requires <code>initramfs8</code>. You can make use of <code>auto_initramfs</code> with custom kernel names provided the names begin with <code>kernel</code> and <code>initramfs</code> respectively and everything else matches (except for the absence of the file extension on the initramfs). Otherwise, an explicit <a href="#initramfs"><code>initramfs</code></a> statement is required.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="disable_poe_fan">
<a class="anchor" href="#disable_poe_fan"></a><code>disable_poe_fan</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>By default, a probe on the I2C bus will happen at startup, even when a PoE HAT is not attached. Setting this option to 1 disables control of a PoE HAT fan through I2C (on pins ID_SD &amp; ID_SC). If you are not intending to use a PoE HAT, this is a helpful way to minimise boot time.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="disable_splash">
<a class="anchor" href="#disable_splash"></a><code>disable_splash</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If <code>disable_splash</code> is set to <code>1</code>, the rainbow splash screen will not be shown on boot. The default value is <code>0</code>.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="enable_uart">
<a class="anchor" href="#enable_uart"></a><code>enable_uart</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>enable_uart=1</code> (in conjunction with <code>console=serial0,115200</code> in <code>cmdline.txt</code>) requests that the kernel creates a serial console, accessible using GPIOs 14 and 15 (pins 8 and 10 on the 40-pin header). Editing <code>cmdline.txt</code> to remove the line <code>quiet</code> enables boot messages from the kernel to also appear there. See also <code>uart_2ndstage</code>.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="force_eeprom_read">
<a class="anchor" href="#force_eeprom_read"></a><code>force_eeprom_read</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Set this option to <code>0</code> to prevent the firmware from trying to read an I2C HAT EEPROM (connected to pins ID_SD &amp; ID_SC) at power up. See also <a href="#disable_poe_fan"><code>disable_poe_fan</code></a>.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="os_prefix">
<a class="anchor" href="#os_prefix"></a><code>os_prefix</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>os_prefix</code> is an optional setting that allows you to choose between multiple versions of the kernel and Device Tree files installed on the same card. Any value in <code>os_prefix</code> is prepended to the name of any operating system files loaded by the firmware, where "operating system files" is defined to mean kernels, <code>initramfs</code>, <code>cmdline.txt</code>, <code>.dtbs</code> and overlays. The prefix would commonly be a directory name, but it could also be part of the filename such as "test-". For this reason, directory prefixes must include the trailing <code>/</code> character.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>In an attempt to reduce the chance of a non-bootable system, the firmware first tests the supplied prefix value for viability – unless the expected kernel and .dtb can be found at the new location/name, the prefix is ignored (set to ""). A special case of this viability test is applied to overlays, which will only be loaded from <code>${os_prefix}${overlay_prefix}</code> (where the default value of <a href="#overlay_prefix"><code>overlay_prefix</code></a> is "overlays/") if <code>${os_prefix}${overlay_prefix}README</code> exists, otherwise it ignores <code>os_prefix</code> and treats overlays as shared.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>(The reason the firmware checks for the existence of key files rather than directories when checking prefixes is twofold: the prefix can’t be a directory, and not all boot methods support testing for the existence of a directory.)</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
Any user-specified OS file can bypass all prefixes by using an absolute path (with respect to the boot partition) – just start the file path with a <code>/</code>, for example, <code>kernel=/my_common_kernel.img</code>.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>See also <a href="#overlay_prefix"><code>overlay_prefix</code></a> and <a href="legacy_config_txt.html#upstream_kernel"><code>upstream_kernel</code></a>.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="otg_mode-raspberry-pi-4-only">
<a class="anchor" href="#otg_mode-raspberry-pi-4-only"></a><code>otg_mode</code> (Raspberry Pi 4 only)</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>USB On-The-Go (often abbreviated to OTG) is a feature that allows supporting USB devices with an appropriate OTG cable to configure themselves as USB hosts. On older Raspberry Pi devices, a single USB 2.0 controller was used in both USB host and device mode.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>A high-performance USB 3.0 controller, attached using PCIe, drives the main USB ports on Raspberry Pi 4B and later, and on Raspberry Pi 400 and later. The legacy USB 2.0 controller is still available on the USB-C power connector for use as a device (<code>otg_mode=0</code>, the default). Raspberry Pi Compute Module 4 and earlier do not include the USB 3.0 controller.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>otg_mode=1</code> requests that a more capable XHCI USB 2.0 controller is used as an alternative host controller on that USB-C connector.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
By default, Raspberry Pi OS includes a line in <code>/boot/firmware/config.txt</code> that enables this setting on Compute Module 4.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="overlay_prefix">
<a class="anchor" href="#overlay_prefix"></a><code>overlay_prefix</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Specifies a subdirectory/prefix from which to load overlays, and defaults to <code>overlays/</code> (note the trailing <code>/</code>). If used in conjunction with <a href="#os_prefix"><code>os_prefix</code></a>, the <code>os_prefix</code> comes before the <code>overlay_prefix</code>; for example, <code>dtoverlay=disable-bt</code> attempts to load <code>${os_prefix}${overlay_prefix}disable-bt.dtbo</code>.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
Unless <code>${os_prefix}${overlay_prefix}README</code> exists, overlays are shared with the main OS (that is, <code>os_prefix</code> is ignored).
</td>
</tr></table></div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="configuration-properties">
<a class="anchor" href="#configuration-properties"></a>Configuration Properties</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Raspberry Pi 5 requires a <code>config.txt</code> file to be present to indicate that the partition is bootable.</p>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="boot_ramdisk">
<a class="anchor" href="#boot_ramdisk"></a><code>boot_ramdisk</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If this property is set to <code>1</code> then the bootloader will attempt load a ramdisk file called <code>boot.img</code> containing the <a href="configuration.html#boot-folder-contents">boot filesystem</a>. Subsequent files (for example, <code>start4.elf</code>) are read from the ramdisk instead of the original boot file system.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The primary purpose of <code>boot_ramdisk</code> is to support <code>secure-boot</code>, however, unsigned <code>boot.img</code> files can also be useful to Network Boot or <code>RPIBOOT</code> configurations.</p>
</div>
<div class="ulist">
<ul><li>
<p>The maximum size for a ramdisk file is 180 MB for Raspberry Pi 4 and later, and 128 MB for earlier devices. However, on devices with limited memory, for example, 256 MB, we recommend that you use a small ramdisk file, for example, 32 MB.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>boot.img</code> files are raw disk <code>.img</code> files. The recommended format is a plain FAT32 partition with no MBR.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>The memory for the ramdisk filesystem is released before the operating system is started.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>If <a href="raspberry-pi.html#fail-safe-os-updates-tryboot">TRYBOOT</a> is selected then the bootloader will search for <code>tryboot.img</code> instead of <code>boot.img</code>.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>See also <a href="#autoboot-txt">autoboot.txt</a>.</p>
</li>
</ul></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>For more information about <code>secure-boot</code> and creating <code>boot.img</code> files, see <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/usbboot/blob/master/Readme.md">USBBOOT</a>.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="boot_load_flags">
<a class="anchor" href="#boot_load_flags"></a><code>boot_load_flags</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Experimental property for custom firmware (bare metal).</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Bit 0 (0x1) indicates that the .elf file is custom firmware. This disables any compatibility checks (for example, is USB MSD boot supported) and resets PCIe before starting the executable.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Not relevant on Raspberry Pi 5 because there is no <code>start.elf</code> file.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0x0</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="enable_rp1_uart">
<a class="anchor" href="#enable_rp1_uart"></a><code>enable_rp1_uart</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Raspberry Pi 5 only.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>When set to <code>1</code>, firmware initialises RP1 UART0 to 115200 bps and doesn’t reset RP1 before starting the OS (separately configurable using <code>pciex4_reset=1</code>).
This makes it easier to get UART output on the 40-pin header in early boot-code, for instance during bare-metal debug.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0x0</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="pciex4_reset">
<a class="anchor" href="#pciex4_reset"></a><code>pciex4_reset</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Raspberry Pi 5 only.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>By default, the PCIe x4 controller used by <code>RP1</code> is reset before starting the operating system. If this parameter is set to <code>0</code> then the reset is disabled allowing operating system or bare metal code to inherit the PCIe configuration setup from the bootloader.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>1</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="sha256">
<a class="anchor" href="#sha256"></a><code>sha256</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If set to non-zero, enables the logging of SHA256 hashes for loaded files (the kernel, initramfs, Device Tree .dtb file, and overlays), as generated by the <code>sha256sum</code> utility. The logging output goes to the UART if enabled, and is also accessible through <code>sudo vclog --msg</code>. This option can be useful when debugging boot problems, but at the cost of potentially adding <em>many</em> seconds to the boot time. Defaults to 0 on all platforms.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="uart_2ndstage">
<a class="anchor" href="#uart_2ndstage"></a><code>uart_2ndstage</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If <code>uart_2ndstage</code> is <code>1</code> then enable debug logging to the UART. This option also automatically enables UART logging in <code>start.elf</code>. This is also described on the <a href="#boot-options">Boot options</a> page.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>BOOT_UART</code> property also enables bootloader UART logging but does not enable UART logging in <code>start.elf</code> unless <code>uart_2ndstage=1</code> is also set.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="erase_eeprom">
<a class="anchor" href="#erase_eeprom"></a><code>erase_eeprom</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If <code>erase_eeprom</code> is set to <code>1</code> then <code>recovery.bin</code> will erase the entire SPI EEPROM instead of flashing the bootloader image. This property has no effect during a normal boot.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="set_reboot_arg1">
<a class="anchor" href="#set_reboot_arg1"></a><code>set_reboot_arg1</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Raspberry Pi 5 only.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Sets the value of <code>boot_arg1</code> to be passed through a reset-safe register to the bootloader after a reboot.
See <a href="#boot_arg1"><code>boot_arg1</code></a> for more details.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>To set the reboot argument at runtime without editing <code>config.txt</code>, you can use <code>vcmailbox</code>. For example, to set <code>boot_arg1</code> to <code>42</code> on the next reboot:</p>
</div>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="console"><span class="gp">$</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nb">sudo </span>vcmailbox 0x0003808c 8 8 1 42</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: ``</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="set_reboot_order">
<a class="anchor" href="#set_reboot_order"></a><code>set_reboot_order</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Raspberry Pi 5 only.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Sets the value of <a href="raspberry-pi.html#BOOT_ORDER">BOOT_ORDER</a> to be passed through a reset-safe register to the bootloader after a reboot. As with <code>tryboot</code>, this is a one-time setting and is automatically cleared after use.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>This property could be used to debug different <a href="raspberry-pi.html#BOOT_ORDER">BOOT_ORDER</a> settings. Alternatively, it could be used in a provisioning system which has control over power and the <code>nRPIBOOT</code> GPIO to override the boot mode without specifying <a href="#conditional-filters">conditional filter</a> statements in the EEPROM config.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>To set the reboot order at runtime without editing <code>config.txt</code>, you can use <code>vcmailbox</code>. For example, to test network boot on the next reboot:</p>
</div>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="console"><span class="gp">$</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nb">sudo </span>vcmailbox 0x0003808b 4 4 0xf4612
<span class="gp">$</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nb">sudo </span>reboot</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: ``</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="kernel_watchdog_timeout">
<a class="anchor" href="#kernel_watchdog_timeout"></a><code>kernel_watchdog_timeout</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If set to a non-zero value (in seconds), this property enables a hardware watchdog timer that is handed over to the operating system (OS) at boot. If the OS does not regularly "kick" or reset the watchdog, the system will be reset after the specified timeout.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>This property sets the <code>systemd</code> <code>watchdog.open_timeout</code> parameter, which controls how long the OS has to initialise and start servicing the watchdog. The value is passed to the OS through the kernel command line. For ongoing operation, the OS must also regularly reset the watchdog, typically controlled by the <code>RuntimeWatchdogSec</code> parameter in <code>systemd</code>. For more information, see <a href="https://www.freedesktop.org/software/systemd/man/latest/systemd-system.conf.html#RuntimeWatchdogSec=">systemd watchdog documentation</a>.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
<div class="paragraph">
<p>On Raspberry Pi OS <em>Bookworm</em>, the <code>RuntimeWatchdogSec</code> parameter is <strong>not enabled by default</strong> and this setting must be configured first in <code>/etc/systemd/system.conf</code> before the firmware kernel watchdog can be used.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If both <code>BOOT_WATCHDOG_TIMEOUT</code> (EEPROM/bootloader setting, only supported on Raspberry Pi 4 and 5) and <code>kernel_watchdog_timeout</code> are set, the bootloader will seamlessly hand over from the bootloader watchdog to the kernel watchdog at the point the OS is started. This provides continuous watchdog coverage from power-on through to OS runtime.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>It is preferred to use <code>kernel_watchdog_timeout</code> rather than <code>dtparam=watchdog</code> because <code>kernel_watchdog_timeout</code> explicitly sets the <code>open_timeout</code> parameter, ensuring the watchdog is active until systemd takes over.</p>
</div>
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>This is useful for ensuring that the system can recover from OS hangs or crashes after the boot process has completed.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0</code> (disabled)</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="kernel_watchdog_partition">
<a class="anchor" href="#kernel_watchdog_partition"></a><code>kernel_watchdog_partition</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If the kernel watchdog triggers (specifically, the OS fails to reset the watchdog within the timeout), this property specifies the partition number to boot from after the reset. This allows for automatic failover to a recovery or alternate partition.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>You can use this in conjunction with the <a href="#the-expression-filter">expression filter</a> to apply different settings or select a different boot flow when the watchdog triggers a reboot to a specific partition.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>See also the <a href="raspberry-pi.html#PARTITION">PARTITION</a> property for more information about how to use high partition numbers to detect a watchdog trigger.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0</code> (default partition)</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="eeprom_write_protect">
<a class="anchor" href="#eeprom_write_protect"></a><code>eeprom_write_protect</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Configures the EEPROM <code>write status register</code>. This can be set either to mark the entire EEPROM as write-protected, or to clear write-protection.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>This option must be used in conjunction with the EEPROM <code>/WP</code> pin which controls updates to the EEPROM <code>Write Status Register</code>.  Pulling <code>/WP</code> low (CM4 <code>EEPROM_nWP</code> or on a Raspberry Pi 4 <code>TP5</code>) does NOT write-protect the EEPROM unless the <code>Write Status Register</code> has also been configured.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>See the <a href="https://www.winbond.com/resource-files/w25x40cl_f%2020140325.pdf">Winbond W25x40cl</a> or <a href="https://www.winbond.com/hq/product/code-storage-flash-memory/serial-nor-flash/?__locale=en&amp;partNo=W25Q16JV">Winbond W25Q16JV</a> datasheets for further details.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>eeprom_write_protect</code> settings in <code>config.txt</code> for <code>recovery.bin</code>.</p>
</div>
<table class="tableblock frame-all grid-all stretch"><colgroup><col style="width: 50%;"><col style="width: 50%;"></colgroup><thead><tr><th class="tableblock halign-left valign-top">Value</th>
<th class="tableblock halign-left valign-top">Description</th>
</tr></thead><tbody><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">1</p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Configures the write protect regions to cover the entire EEPROM.</p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">0</p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Clears the write protect regions.</p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">-1</p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Do nothing.</p></td>
</tr></tbody></table><div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
<code>flashrom</code> does not support clearing of the write-protect regions and will fail to update the EEPROM if write-protect regions are defined.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>On Raspberry Pi 5 <code>/WP</code> is pulled low by default and consequently write-protect is enabled as soon as the <code>Write Status Register</code> is configured. To clear write-protect pull <code>/WP</code> high by connecting <code>TP14</code> and <code>TP1</code>.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>-1</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="os_check">
<a class="anchor" href="#os_check"></a><code>os_check</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>On Raspberry Pi 5 the firmware automatically checks for a compatible Device Tree file before attempting to boot from the current partition. Otherwise, older non-compatible kernels would be loaded and then hang.
To disable this check (for example, for bare-metal development), set <code>os_check=0</code> in config.txt</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>1</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="bootloader_update">
<a class="anchor" href="#bootloader_update"></a><code>bootloader_update</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>You can set this option to 0 to block self-update without requiring the EEPROM configuration to be updated. This is sometimes useful when updating multiple Raspberry Pi devices through network boot because this option can be controlled per Raspberry Pi (for example, by using a serial number filter in <code>config.txt</code>).</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>1</code></p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="secure-boot-configuration-properties">
<a class="anchor" href="#secure-boot-configuration-properties"></a>Secure Boot configuration properties</h3>
<div class="sidebarblock whitepaper"><div class="content">
<div class="title"><h5><a href="https://pip.raspberrypi.com/documents/RP-003466-WP" target="_blank">How to use Raspberry Pi Secure Boot</a></h5></div>
<div class="inner">
<div class="coverimage" data-link="https://pip.raspberrypi.com/documents/RP-003466-WP">
<p class="title">How to use Raspberry Pi Secure Boot</p>
<p class="subtitle"></p>
</div>
<div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>This whitepaper describes how to implement secure boot on devices based on Raspberry Pi 4. For an overview of our approach to implementing secure boot, see the <a href="https://pip.raspberrypi.com/documents/RP-004651-WP">Raspberry Pi 4 Boot Security</a> whitepaper.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The secure boot system is intended for use with custom OS images such as those generated with <code>rpi-image-gen</code> or <code>buildroot</code>. Using it with Raspberry Pi OS isn’t recommended.</p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
</div></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The following <code>config.txt</code> properties are used to program the <code>secure-boot</code> OTP settings. These changes are irreversible and can only be programmed by using <code>RPIBOOT</code> when flashing the bootloader EEPROM image. This ensures that <code>secure-boot</code> cannot be set remotely or by accidentally inserting a stale SD card image.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>For more information about enabling <code>secure-boot</code>, see the following resources:</p>
</div>
<div class="ulist">
<ul><li>
<p><a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/usbboot/blob/master/Readme.md#secure-boot">Secure Boot readme</a> and the <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/usbboot/blob/master/secure-boot-example/README.md">Secure Boot tutorial</a> in the <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/usbboot">USBBOOT</a> repo.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><a href="../security/security-architecture.html#secure-boot-chain-of-trust">Secure Boot Chain of Trust</a>.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><a href="../security/secure-boot-provisioner.html">Secure Boot Provisioner (<code>rpi-sb-provisioner</code>)</a>.</p>
</li>
</ul></div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="program_pubkey">
<a class="anchor" href="#program_pubkey"></a><code>program_pubkey</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If this property is set to <code>1</code> then <code>recovery.bin</code> will write the hash of the public key in the EEPROM image to OTP.  When set, the bootloader rejects EEPROM images signed with different RSA keys or unsigned images.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="revoke_devkey">
<a class="anchor" href="#revoke_devkey"></a><code>revoke_devkey</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Raspberry Pi 4 only.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If this property is set to <code>1</code> then <code>recovery.bin</code> will write a value to OTP that prevents the ROM from loading old versions of the second stage bootloader which do not support <code>secure-boot</code>. This prevents <code>secure-boot</code> from being turned off by reverting to an older release of the bootloader. Therefore, this property must be set if <code>secure-boot</code> is enabled on production devices.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>This property is automatically set by <code>recovery.bin</code> release version <code>2025/05/16</code> and later if <code>program_pubkey=1</code>.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0</code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="program_rpiboot_gpio">
<a class="anchor" href="#program_rpiboot_gpio"></a><code>program_rpiboot_gpio</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Raspberry Pi 4B and Raspberry Pi 400 only.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Compute Module 4 and 4S have a dedicated <code>nRPIBOOT</code> jumper to select <code>RPIBOOT</code> mode. Raspberry Pi 4B and Raspberry Pi 400 lack a dedicated <code>nRPIBOOT</code> jumper so one of the following GPIOs must be selected for use as <code>nRPIBOOT</code>.</p>
</div>
<div class="ulist">
<ul><li>
<p><code>2</code></p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>4</code></p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>5</code></p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>6</code></p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>7</code></p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>8</code></p>
</li>
</ul></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>You can use the GPIO as a general-purpose I/O pin after the OS has started. However, you should verify that this GPIO configuration does not conflict with any HATs which might pull the GPIO low during boot.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Although <code>secure-boot</code> requires this property to be set on Raspberry Pi 4B and Raspberry Pi 400, it does not depend on <code>secure-boot</code>. For example, <code>RPIBOOT</code> can be useful for automated testing.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>For safety, this OTP value can <em>only</em> be programmed by using <code>RPIBOOT</code>. As a result, you must first clear the bootloader EEPROM using <code>erase_eeprom</code>. The blank EEPROM causes the ROM to failover to <code>RPIBOOT</code> mode, which then allows this option to be set.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code> </code></p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect3">
<h4 id="program_jtag_lock">
<a class="anchor" href="#program_jtag_lock"></a><code>program_jtag_lock</code>
</h4>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>If this property is set to <code>1</code> then <code>recovery.bin</code> will program an OTP value that prevents VideoCore JTAG from being used. This option requires that <code>program_pubkey</code> and <code>revoke_devkey</code> are also set. This option can prevent failure analysis, and should only be set after the device has been fully tested.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Default: <code>0</code></p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect1">
<h2 id="gpio-control">
<a class="anchor" href="#gpio-control"></a>GPIO control</h2>
<div class="sectionbody"></div>
<div class="paragraph edit-link">
<p>Edit this <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/documentation/blob/master/documentation/asciidoc/computers/config_txt/gpio.adoc">on GitHub</a></p>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="gpio">
<a class="anchor" href="#gpio"></a><code>gpio</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>gpio</code> directive allows GPIO pins to be set to specific modes and values at boot time in a way that would previously have needed a custom <code>dt-blob.bin</code> file. Each line applies the same settings (or at least makes the same changes) to a set of pins, addressing either a single pin (<code>3</code>), a range of pins (<code>3-4</code>), or a comma-separated list of either (<code>3-4,6,8</code>).</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The pin set is followed by an <code>=</code> and one or more comma-separated attributes from this list:</p>
</div>
<div class="ulist">
<ul><li>
<p><code>ip</code> – Input</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>op</code> – Output</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>a0-a5</code> – Alt0-Alt5</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>dh</code> – Driving high (for outputs)</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>dl</code> – Driving low (for outputs)</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>pu</code> – Pull up</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>pd</code> – Pull down</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><code>pn/np</code> – No pull</p>
</li>
</ul></div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p><code>gpio</code> settings apply in order, so those appearing later override those appearing earlier.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Examples:</p>
</div>
<div class="listingblock">
<button class="copy-button hidden" aria-label="copy code to clipboard" type="button" aria-hidden="true"><div class="copy-button-inner">
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" id="Layer_1" data-name="Layer 1" viewbox="0 0 12 12"><rect x="1" y="6" width="5" height="1"></rect><path d="M10,1H8A1,1,0,0,0,7,0H6A1,1,0,0,0,5,1H3A1,1,0,0,0,2,2V5H3V2H4V3H9V2h1v9H3V8H2v3a1,1,0,0,0,1,1h7a1,1,0,0,0,1-1V2A1,1,0,0,0,10,1ZM6.5,2.25a.75.75,0,1,1,.75-.75A.76.76,0,0,1,6.5,2.25Z"></path><path d="M6.32,6.32,4.43,4.43A.25.25,0,0,0,4,4.6V8.4a.25.25,0,0,0,.43.17L6.32,6.68A.25.25,0,0,0,6.32,6.32Z"></path></svg><strong class="copy-button-label"></strong><span class="tooltip hidden"> Copy to Clipboard</span>
</div></button>
<div class="content">
<pre class="rouge highlight"><code data-lang="ini"><span class="c"># Select Alt2 for GPIO pins 0 to 27 (for DPI24)
</span><span class="py">gpio</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">0-27=a2</span>
<span class="w">
</span><span class="c"># Set GPIO12 to be an output set to 1
</span><span class="py">gpio</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">12=op,dh</span>
<span class="w">
</span><span class="c"># Change the pull on (input) pins 18 and 20
</span><span class="py">gpio</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">18,20=pu</span>
<span class="w">
</span><span class="c"># Make pins 17 to 21 inputs
</span><span class="py">gpio</span><span class="p">=</span><span class="s">17-21=ip</span></code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The <code>gpio</code> directive respects the "[…​]" conditional filters in <code>config.txt</code>, so it is possible to use different settings based on the model, serial number, and EDID.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>GPIO changes made through this mechanism do not have any direct effect on the kernel. They don’t cause GPIO pins to be exported to the <code>sysfs</code> interface, and they can be overridden by <code>pinctrl</code> entries in the Device Tree as well as utilities like <code>pinctrl</code>.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Note also that there is a delay of a few seconds between power being applied and the changes taking effect – longer if booting over the network or from a USB mass storage device.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="enable_jtag_gpio">
<a class="anchor" href="#enable_jtag_gpio"></a><code>enable_jtag_gpio</code>
</h3>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Setting <code>enable_jtag_gpio=1</code> selects Alt4 mode for GPIO pins 22-27, and sets up some internal SoC connections, enabling the JTAG interface for the Arm CPU. It works on all models of Raspberry Pi.</p>
</div>
<table class="tableblock frame-all grid-all stretch"><colgroup><col style="width: 50%;"><col style="width: 50%;"></colgroup><thead><tr><th class="tableblock halign-left valign-top">Pin #</th>
<th class="tableblock halign-left valign-top">Function</th>
</tr></thead><tbody><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">GPIO22</p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>ARM_TRST</code></p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">GPIO23</p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>ARM_RTCK</code></p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">GPIO24</p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>ARM_TDO</code></p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">GPIO25</p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>ARM_TCK</code></p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">GPIO26</p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>ARM_TDI</code></p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">GPIO27</p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>ARM_TMS</code></p></td>
</tr></tbody></table></div>
</div>
<div class="sect1">
<h2 id="overclocking-options">
<a class="anchor" href="#overclocking-options"></a>Overclocking options</h2>
<div class="sectionbody"></div>
<div class="paragraph edit-link">
<p>Edit this <a href="https://github.com/raspberrypi/documentation/blob/master/documentation/asciidoc/computers/config_txt/overclocking.adoc">on GitHub</a></p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>The kernel has a <a href="https://www.kernel.org/doc/html/latest/admin-guide/pm/cpufreq.html">CPUFreq</a> driver with the powersave governor enabled by default, switched to on-demand during boot, when <a href="configuration.html#raspi-config">raspi-config</a> is installed. With the on-demand governor, CPU frequency will vary with processor load. You can adjust the minimum values with the <code>*_min</code> config options, or disable dynamic clocking by applying a static scaling governor (powersave or performance) or with <code>force_turbo=1</code>.</p>
</div>
<div class="paragraph">
<p>Overclocking and overvoltage will be disabled at runtime when the SoC reaches <code>temp_limit</code> (see below), which defaults to 85°C, in order to cool down the SoC. You should not hit this limit with Raspberry Pi 1 and Raspberry Pi 2, but you are more likely to with Raspberry Pi 3 and newer. Overclocking and overvoltage are also disabled when an undervoltage situation is detected.</p>
</div>
<div class="admonitionblock note">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Note</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
For more information <a href="raspberry-pi.html#frequency-management-and-thermal-control">see the section on frequency management and thermal control</a>.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="admonitionblock warning">
<table><tr><td class="icon">
<div class="title">Warning</div>
</td>
<td class="content">
Setting any overclocking parameters to values other than those used by <a href="configuration.html#overclock"><code>raspi-config</code></a> might set a permanent bit within the SoC. This makes it possible to detect that your Raspberry Pi was once overclocked. The overclock bit sets when <code>force_turbo</code> is set to <code>1</code> and any of the <code>over_voltage_*</code> options are set to a value of more than <code>0</code>. See the <a href="https://www.raspberrypi.com/news/introducing-turbo-mode-up-to-50-more-performance-for-free/">blog post on Turbo mode</a> for more information.
</td>
</tr></table></div>
<div class="sect2">
<h3 id="overclocking">
<a class="anchor" href="#overclocking"></a>Overclocking</h3>
<table class="tableblock frame-all grid-all stretch"><colgroup><col style="width: 25%;"><col style="width: 75%;"></colgroup><thead><tr><th class="tableblock halign-left valign-top">Option</th>
<th class="tableblock halign-left valign-top">Description</th>
</tr></thead><tbody><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>arm_freq</code></p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Frequency of the Arm CPU in MHz.</p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>arm_boost</code></p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Increases <code>arm_freq</code> to the highest supported frequency for the board-type and firmware. Set to <code>1</code> to enable.</p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>gpu_freq</code></p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Sets <code>core_freq</code>, <code>h264_freq</code>, <code>isp_freq</code>, <code>v3d_freq</code> and <code>hevc_freq</code> together.</p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>core_freq</code></p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Frequency of the GPU processor core in MHz. Influences CPU performance because it drives the L2 cache and memory bus; the L2 cache benefits only Raspberry Pi Zero/Raspberry Pi Zero W/Raspberry Pi 1; and there is a small benefit for SDRAM on Raspberry Pi 2 and Raspberry Pi 3. See section below for use on Raspberry Pi 4.</p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>h264_freq</code></p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Frequency of the hardware video block in MHz; individual override of the <code>gpu_freq</code> setting.</p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>isp_freq</code></p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Frequency of the image sensor pipeline block in MHz; individual override of the <code>gpu_freq</code> setting.</p></td>
</tr><tr><td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock"><code>v3d_freq</code></p></td>
<td class="tableblock halign-left valign-top"><p class="tableblock">Frequency of the 3D block in MHz; individual override of the <code>gpu_freq</code> setting. On Raspberry Pi 5, V3D is independent of <code>core_freq</code>, <code>isp_freq</code>  and <code>hevc_freq</code>.</p></td>
